\part{The click model: the measurement chapter}\label{part:click}

\section{The click model}\label{sec:model}

\paragraph{The model in plain words.} The GameBoard is a mail board.
Cells (Nodes), each wired to six neighbours; a wire carries one packet one
step per tick, and nothing travels farther or faster. A packet carries
four numbers that matter here, how much (the amount), where to (the
direction), a rotating counter (the phase, advanced a fixed step at every
wire it crosses) and a birth number, and its bookkeeping (a label and a
multiplicity, Definition~\ref{def:row}). A cell has no memory: what is in it now is exactly the
packets in it now. The rules are local and few. Transfer: a packet moves
to the neighbour its direction names. Duplication at a splitter: a packet
becomes several packets in several directions with declared shares, the
birth number the same on all. Merging: identical packets add; identical
packets with opposite counters cancel and leave nothing. Deflection
beside a mass: a packet's direction is turned by a table, and the turn
can be undone (no world of this chapter has a mass). The collector, the
apparatus, keeps per birth number and per cell the running sum of the
packets' arrows. When every packet of a birth has ended, the collector
lays the cells on a ruler in proportion to the squared lengths of those
sums; a secret number born with the birth, the counter every packet of
the birth started from and which no rule reads as a number, picks the
point on the ruler; the whole birth is recorded at that cell. For a birth
that reaches two collectors with two settings, the collectors' record is
one object: the choice is made once, when the packets at both have
ended, from the sums at both with both settings, and the near
collector's row is written at the far collector's tick. That one choice
from both settings is the only action at a distance in the model;
nothing else crosses a wire faster than a packet. Births happen only at
sources, on their own clocks; deaths only at clicks. Everything between
a birth and its click can be run backwards, given the collectors' record
of what they absorbed.

\paragraph{What was asked, and what came before.} The question of this
chapter is the one a previous paper on the same simulator asked of four
candidate mechanisms \cite{paper1}: which resource does an integer,
local, causal model need to reproduce the correlations of one quantum and
of a pair. There, every candidate whose information travelled only on
Links stopped at the Bell bound, $S = 2$ exactly, also when the settings
were chosen by events of the GameBoard itself (no superdeterministic
correlation was found), and only a declared shared object, a registry
holding a configured cosine law, reached $2.83$, as that paper reported
(its runs are in its own archive, not in the current register). That
framework is the ``before'' of this paper and is not repeated; its engine
of the time was replaced and none of its tables is reproduced. Here the
registry and the
configured law are gone. What remains is the GameBoard, one record with
rows, and one measurement rule, the click of Definition~\ref{def:click}.
This chapter defines the model in standard terms (the words collapse
and superposition appear first in Section~\ref{sec:lit}, each as another
author's name for one of these things), proves what can be proved of it
(Section~\ref{sec:theorems}), reports what the engine measured, from
runs recorded before this text was written (Section~\ref{sec:measure}),
compares the finite-$\Nphi$ Bell value with the experiments
(Section~\ref{sec:bell}), places the click in the causal anatomy of
Bell's theorem (Section~\ref{sec:causal}), and says what is new in it,
what it does not claim and what the program hypothesised beside it at
the time of its runs (Sections~\ref{sec:new} to \ref{sec:hyp}). All
code, worlds and recorded runs are archived \cite{zenodo}.


\paragraph{In one sentence.} The model is the law of Part~I on worlds
without bodies; the measurement is the one threshold that is read out.
The state is a vector of integers, and in the worlds of this chapter
every interval applies three of the six operations to it: it translates
the vector by a rate and takes what crossed a wall as the events (the
flight, the phase, every count and the birth), it multiplies by a
declared integer matrix (the split and the rotation, exact and
reversible), and it adds (the merge, where equal phases sum and opposite
phases cancel). Between clicks the rates are
constant, so the map has a closed form, and every count of the
measurements table is computed from it by the design's check scripts
before its run, the two slits excepted (its expectation is the
generator's reading of a one-birth reference world through the same
geometry on the engine, re-pinned once after a first run,
Section~\ref{sec:measure}); the departures of Section~\ref{sec:measure}
are what the runs added. (The engine also holds a collision table and a push of matter
by a row's share, $\mathrm{amount}^2/\mathtt m$; neither acts in these worlds. The
flight table is computed once at load.) The click is the one place with a fourth
operation: the apparatus sums a record's rows through the tables and
\emph{squares} the sum (the norm, where the Born rule is put in), and only
then applies its threshold to $u$; it is the one threshold whose crossing
is written down, and it is done with the record it read (the archived
code then releases it).

\paragraph{The GameBoard and the circle.} Nodes are the points of a box in
$\Z^3$ (an axis may be periodic); each Node has six Links. Time is the
interval $t \in \Z$. The circle is $\Z_{\Nphi}$ with $4 \mid \Nphi$. The tables are
$C_{\Nphi}[p] = \mathrm{round}(256\cos(2\pi p/\Nphi))$ and $S_{\Nphi}[p] = C_{\Nphi}[p - \Nphi/4]$,
so $C_{\Nphi}[p + \Nphi/2] = -C_{\Nphi}[p]$ and $S_{\Nphi}[p + \Nphi/4] = C_{\Nphi}[p]$ exactly; the norm
$C_{\Nphi}^2 + S_{\Nphi}^2$ lies in $[65536 - 351,\ 65536 + 361]$ for every power of
two $\Nphi \le 65536$ (the extremes $65185$ and $65897$, the latter at
$\Nphi = 4096$, $p = 503$ and $3593$: $C = 184$, $|S| = 179$; at $\Nphi = 64$ the range is
$-88$ to $+237$) \cite{checks}. The half-angle tables are $C'_{\Nphi} = C_{2\Nphi}$,
$S'_{\Nphi} = S_{2\Nphi}$. Directions $D$ are the six headings and declared
primitive integer vectors; the flight table gives, per direction $D$ and
age $\tau$, the Node offset $\mathrm{pos}_D(\tau)$ on the digital line of
$D$, at most one Link per interval, and Euclidean speed $N_l|D|/T_D$ with
$T_D = \lfloor\sqrt{3|D|^2N_l^2}\rfloor$ and the flight scale $N_l = 64$:
within $0.8$ percent of $1/\sqrt3$ for every $D$, exact on $(1,1,1)$
\cite{beamlaw}.

\paragraph{The pace of the rows, and the octahedron.} Two rules are the
whole kinematics of a row: it crosses at most one Link per interval (the
locality of the GameBoard), and it follows the digital line of its
direction, so that after one period of $S_1 = |D_x| + |D_y| + |D_z|$
Links its displacement is the vector $D$ itself (straightness). The Nodes
one interval away from a Node are the six neighbours at the ends of its
Links, the vertices of the octahedron $|x| + |y| + |z| \le 1$
(Figure~\ref{fig:octahedron}); the octahedron scaled by the age is the
causal front, inside which every row stays. A row's mean Euclidean pace
lies within $1/T_D$ of the octahedron's inscribed sphere
(Proposition~\ref{prop:pace}), so the vertex's pace, one Link per
interval along a heading, is reached by no row's mean pace: the front is
a bound and not a signal.

\begin{proposition}[The pace]\label{prop:pace}
For every primitive direction $D$, $S_1 N_l \le T_D$: the Manhattan pace
$S_1 N_l/T_D$ is at most one Link per interval on every digital line, and
$S_1 = \sqrt3\,|D|$ exactly when $|D_x| = |D_y| = |D_z|$. A pace $v$ that
is the same in every direction and at most one Link per interval on every
line satisfies $v \le |D|/S_1$ for every $D$, hence $v \le 1/\sqrt3$, the
radius of the octahedron's inscribed sphere; and the table's Euclidean
pace obeys $1/\sqrt3 \le N_l|D|/T_D < (1/\sqrt3)(1 + 1/T_D)$ for every $D$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
$S_1^2 = (|D_x| + |D_y| + |D_z|)^2 \le 3(D_x^2 + D_y^2 + D_z^2)$ is the
Cauchy--Schwarz inequality against $(1, 1, 1)$, an equality exactly when
the three sizes are equal; so $S_1 N_l \le \sqrt{3|D|^2N_l^2}$, and since
$S_1 N_l$ is an integer, $S_1 N_l \le \lfloor\sqrt{3|D|^2N_l^2}\rfloor =
T_D$. A row at the Euclidean pace $v$ on the line of $D$ makes $S_1$
Links per length $|D|$, so $vS_1/|D|$ Links per interval on average, and
at most one Link per interval bounds this by $1$: $v \le |D|/S_1$. The
least of $|D|/S_1$ over directions is $1/\sqrt3$, on the cube diagonals
($1/\sqrt n$ in $n$ dimensions). $T_D \le \sqrt3\,N_l|D| < T_D + 1$ gives
the pace's interval.
\end{proof}

So $c = 1/\sqrt3$ Links per interval is the largest isotropic pace the two
rules allow, and the table sits at it; that it sits at the supremum and
not below is the flight's declared wall $T_D = \lfloor\sqrt{3|D|^2
N_l^2}\rfloor$, the design's third statement, a rule chosen among few
(P9; the derivation's 24.1, rows 7 and 9), not a consequence of the
two; given the wall the value is the flight operator's norm, so the
value rests on locality, straightness and the declared wall. Stated exactly: the two rules give
$c \le 1/\sqrt3$ (one Link per interval allows the pace $1$ on a heading
and only $1/\sqrt3$ on a cube diagonal, three Links for the displacement
$\sqrt3$), and what is derived is the largest uniform pace the GameBoard
allows under them; why the rows realise it, and what a detector in
motion reads of it, are not derived here (Section~\ref{sec:lorentz}:
a one-way count of a stream reads $1 \mp \beta$, $\beta$ (beta) the
detector's speed as a fraction of $c$). Over the $1\,780\,418$ primitive
directions with components within $64$ the bound holds on every one, with
the integer equality $S_1 N_l = T_D$ on $2072$: the $8$ cube diagonals,
and $2064$ directions near them where the floor closes a gap below
$1/N_l$ (the shortest $(22, 21, 21)$, $S_1 = 64$, $\sqrt3|D| = 64.016$);
on all of these the row crosses exactly one Link per interval
(computation, \texttt{light\_speed.txt}; the design's map counts the same
$1\,780\,418$ and $2072$ and describes the $2072$ as cube diagonals, which
is corrected here \cite{lightspeed}). The pace is $64/110 = 0.5818$ on a
heading, $0.77$ percent above $1/\sqrt3$, $0.49$ percent above on the
plane diagonal, exact on the cube diagonal; the anisotropy is the table's
rounding, below $1/T_D$ in every direction and so of order $1/(\sqrt3
N_l)$; a measured bound on the anisotropy of $c$ in the GameBoard's frame
puts $N_l$ at or above its inverse, of order $5.8 \times 10^{17}$ for
$10^{-18}$ \cite{nagel2015} (a two-arm interferometer at rest with both
arms on headings sees nothing, the two paces being equal by the $48$; the
rotating resonators of \cite{nagel2015} would see the cubic pattern to the
same order; what the law says of a laboratory in motion is
Section~\ref{sec:lorentz}). Nothing measured enters the value. The number
is, as is well known, the bound of the Courant condition of the standard
second-order scheme for the wave equation, $c\,\Delta t/\Delta x \le
1/\sqrt n$ \cite{cfl1928}, whose necessity is this same argument (the
sphere of radius $c\,\Delta t$ inside the stencil's octahedron), and the
lattice Boltzmann sound speed $c_s^2 = 1/3$, from the isotropy of the
lattice tensor \cite{qian1992}. What the paper adds is the exact integer
table sitting at the bound, its rounding's anisotropy as a bound on the
grain, and the program's intent of one operator for rows and bodies (a
body's drive as the same operator at a fraction: decided and in build,
not run \cite{log}); it claims no novelty for the number
(Section~\ref{sec:lit}). The symmetry of the octahedron and of the flight
is the group of the $48$ signed permutations of the axes ($3! \times 2^3$;
$24$ of determinant $+1$, the rotations, and $24$ of $-1$, the
reflections), the GameBoard's stand-in for the rotation group: $T_D$ and
$S_1$ are invariant under all $48$, the digital line's tie by axis order
is not (on $(1, 1, 0)$, for half of the $48$, the line of $gd$ and $g$
applied to the line of $D$ differ at the odd Links and agree at every
whole period; on $(1, 0, 0)$ and $(2, 1, 0)$ they agree everywhere
\cite{checks}), so the flight commutes with the $48$ at the periods and
not between them. No body moves in the worlds of this chapter.

\paragraph{No dispersion.} What the GameBoard does not do to the pace is
as definite as what it does.

\begin{remark}[The pace is the direction's]\label{rem:nodispersion}
By Definition~\ref{def:rules} the flight table is indexed by $(D, \tau)$,
the phase is a separate accumulator, and no rule of the interval (the
flight, the birth, the split, the rotation, the merge; the archived
collision table reads no phase) reads a phase into a direction or a
step. So a row's pace
is $N_l|D|/T_D$ at every phase rate up to the alias bound of $\Nphi/2$
steps per interval (a turn every two intervals, $1.15$ to $1.16$ Links),
and the anisotropy of Proposition~\ref{prop:pace} is the same at every
rate. This is the pure-shift case of a lattice automaton
\cite{meyer1996}: a ballistic walker with a passenger phase, free of
dispersion by construction, as a classical corpuscle is. What is not
trivial is the pair with Proposition~\ref{prop:pace}: a transport on
$\Z^3$ that is isotropic within $1/T_D$ in every direction and free of
dispersion in every direction, while the carried phase interferes at the
click. No local linear wave scheme on the GameBoard can be both: its
dispersion relation, $\omega$ the frequency and $k$ the wave vector, is
a polynomial equation in $e^{i\omega\Delta t}$ and $e^{ik_ja}$ ($a$ the
Link, $\Delta t$ the interval's length), so every branch $\omega(k)$ is
periodic in $k$ up to multiples of $2\pi/\Delta t$ while $c|k|$ is not,
and $\omega = c|k|$ holds on no open set of $k$.
\end{remark}

The price is stated with it: the model carries no wave on the GameBoard
(the interference is summed in the record, Definition~\ref{def:click}),
and no row carries an energy, so a photon enters only by a reading:
a photon of energy $E$ is read as a family whose phase turns the
fraction $E\,\Delta t/h$ of the circle per interval, $\Nphi E\,\Delta t/h$
steps of $\Z_{\Nphi}$, $h$ Planck's constant (assumed); the alias bound is then
$E\,\Delta t/h < 1/2$.
Under that reading the model's light has one speed at every energy, the
null of continuum physics, which distinguishes it among lattice
transports only; one measured energy dependence of $c$ refutes the
flight as defined. The numbers (computation, \texttt{light\_speed.txt}):
the Link is bounded by the shortest wavelength seen, a bound every
lattice model shares, wave or walker: the $1.4$ PeV photon of
\cite{lhaaso2021}, of wavelength $\Lambda = 8.9 \times 10^{-22}$ m,
gives $a \le \Lambda\sqrt3/2 = 7.7 \times 10^{-22}$ m under the pair
form of the phase and $a \le \Lambda/\sqrt2 = 6.3 \times 10^{-22}$ m
under the integer form (two Links spanning $\sqrt2\,a$ on a cube
diagonal's line); the bound moves with the record. The time-of-flight
tests bound nothing here and bound a lattice wave by its scheme and its
direction: the standard second-order scheme at the Courant number
$1/\sqrt3$ has, on an axis, the group velocity $v_g = c\,(1 - (ka)^2/12
+ \dots)$ and $v_g = 0$ at the band edge $ka = \pi$, and is exactly
dispersionless on the cube diagonal; one photon of $31$ GeV arriving
within $0.83$ s of the trigger of a burst at the redshift $z = 0.903$
\cite{abdo2009},
over the comoving distance $9.5 \times 10^{25}$ m of that paper's
cosmology without the cosmological weighting (conservative), needs
$|v_g - c|/c < 2.6 \times 10^{-18}$, which puts that scheme's Link on an
axis below $3.6 \times 10^{-26}$ m and a fourth-order scheme's, taking
$(ka)^4/100$ for its leading term (an order of magnitude, not a scheme's
coefficient), below $8 \times 10^{-22}$ m. The two bounds come from two
instruments, and a lattice wave obeys both.

\begin{figure}[H]\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.5\textwidth]{figures/octahedron.pdf}
\caption{\label{fig:octahedron}The Nodes one interval from a Node. The six
neighbours at the ends of the six Links (the Ports $\pm x$, $\pm y$,
$\pm z$) are the vertices of the octahedron $|x| + |y| + |z| \le 1$, the
causal front of one interval. Its inscribed sphere, of radius $1/\sqrt3$,
touches the eight faces on the cube diagonals at $(\pm1, \pm1, \pm1)/3$
and is the rows' pace $c$, the largest isotropic pace under one Link per
interval (Proposition~\ref{prop:pace}); a row's mean pace lies within
$1/T_D$ of the sphere and never at a vertex.
The faint cube shares the octahedron's symmetry, the $48$ signed
permutations of the axes, $3! \times 2^3$, $24$ rotations and $24$
reflections told apart by the determinant, the GameBoard's stand-in for the
rotation group. Drawn by \texttt{octahedron.py} from the definitions; no
run.}
\end{figure}

\begin{definition}[Row and record]\label{def:row}
A \emph{row} is $(x, D, \tau, p, e, h, r, \ell, w, \mathtt m)$: Node, direction,
age, phase $p \in \Z_{\Nphi}$, emitter, content per unit, record $r$, branch
label $\ell$, amount $w \ge 1$, multiplicity $\mathtt m \ge 1$. Its amplitude is
\begin{equation}
z = \frac{w}{\sqrt{\mathtt m}}\,\frac{C_{\Nphi}[p] + i S_{\Nphi}[p]}{256}, \qquad |z|^2 = \frac{w^2}{m}\cdot\frac{C_{\Nphi}[p]^2 + S_{\Nphi}[p]^2}{65536}.
\end{equation}
Two rows equal in every field but $w$ are one row with the amounts added;
two rows of one record and label equal in every field but a phase
difference of $\Nphi/2$ are one row with the amounts subtracted (an equal pair
is no row). The state of record $r$ is an element $\psi_r$ of the free
$\Z$-module $M_r$ on the basis $(x, D, \tau, p \bmod \Nphi/2, e, h, \ell, \mathtt m)$
with $|p + \Nphi/2\rangle = -|p\rangle$, taken modulo the scaling
$(w, \mathtt m) \sim (kw, k^2 \mathtt m)$; the GameBoard's state is one normal form per
element of $\bigoplus_r M_r$. The norm is $\langle\psi,\psi\rangle =
\sum_{\text{rows}} w^2/\mathtt m$. The archived engine narrows the quotient at a
detector: rows of one record with different $\mathtt m$ at one set are added when
the ratio of the multiplicities is the square of a rational (the pointers
rescaled by the integer roots) and refused otherwise; at the tree of the
runs two multiplicities at one set were refused, and no reported world
has them, so every set below reads one $\mathtt m$.
\end{definition}

The host, not any Node, keeps per live record the birth phase $u \in \Z_{\Nphi}$,
the count of live units (amounts), an accumulated pointer $(X, Y)$ per
(Node, label) and a gathered flag. This is the apparatus's layer; the GameBoard never reads it. It is a memory no Node could own: on the two-slit
world the two paths of one record reach a pixel up to $18$ intervals
apart, so no Node ever holds both rows, and the coherent sum exists only
in the record's ledger \cite{log}.

\begin{definition}[The update rules of one interval]\label{def:rules}
Applied in this order to every row.
\begin{enumerate}
\item[F] Flight: $(x, D, \tau, p) \mapsto (x + \mathrm{pos}_d(\tau+1) -
\mathrm{pos}_D(\tau),\ d,\ \tau + 1,\ p + \varphi(\tau))$, where the
family's phase turn is either an integer per Link, added only when the
step is a Link, or a pair $[n, d]$ per interval of age,
$\varphi(\tau) = \lfloor (\tau+1) n/d \rfloor - \lfloor \tau n/d \rfloor$,
counted from the row's birth or last re-emission.
\item[C] Collision: at a Node holding several rows of amount $1$ the
table permutes their directions as a function of the whole set present;
$r, \ell, \mathtt m$ unchanged; a bijection of states, not a linear map. No
collision acts in any world of this paper.
\item[S] Split, at a measured event with outputs $\{(d_i, a_i, t_i)\}$,
$a_i \in \Z$: the row $(w, \mathtt m, p)$ is absorbed and re-emitted as the rows
$(w a_i,\ \mathtt m A,\ p + t_i)$ on $d_i$ at age $0$, $A = \sum_i a_i^2$. The
balanced splitter is $(1, 1)$, $A = 2$; the reflection's turn is $\Nphi/4$.
\item[R] Rotation, at a set with setting $s$ and turn $t$, on a label
pair $(0, 1)$ read into the channels $(+, -)$:
$U_{s,t} = \begin{pmatrix} C'[s] & S'[s]\,v(t) \\ -S'[s] & C'[s]\,v(t)\end{pmatrix}$
with $v(t)$ the turn by $t$ steps on the label-$1$ column; a row of
label $0$ becomes $(wC'[s], 65536\mathtt m, p)$ on $+$ and $(wS'[s], 65536\mathtt m,
p + \Nphi/2)$ on $-$, a row of label $1$ becomes $(wS'[s], 65536\mathtt m, p + t)$ on
$+$ and $(wC'[s], 65536\mathtt m, p + t)$ on $-$ (the row form exact for $t$ a
multiple of $\Nphi/4$, the only turns used here; the archived code multiplies
the column by the tables' phasor at $t$). With $t = 0$ this is $U_s$ of
Theorem~\ref{th:isometry}; the GHZ bases below are $X = (s, 0)$ and
$Y = (s, \Nphi/4)$.
\item[M] Normal form: merge and cancel as in Definition~\ref{def:row}.
\item[B] Birth: a lamp's self-creation at interval $t$ releases record $r$
with $u$ the lamp's phase at the birth, $(b - 1) \bmod \Nphi$ for a lamp
started at $0$ that turns one step per birth, $b$ its count of births
(every world here; $b - 1 = t - 1$ only while the lamp births at every
interval; the archived code writes $(b - 1) \bmod \Nphi$ directly), rows of
amount $1$ on its $k$
directions with $\mathtt m = k$, each row's phase starting at $u$ (plus the
lamp's own turn), and, for a pair, the declared labels with integer
weights on separate arms.
\end{enumerate}
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[The click]\label{def:click}
At a set that reads the sum, the rows of record $r$ and label $\ell$ that
arrive are absorbed and the layer accumulates
\begin{equation}
X \mathrel{+}= \sum 32\,w\,C_{\Nphi}[p],\qquad Y \mathrel{+}= \sum 32\,w\,S_{\Nphi}[p],
\end{equation}
the phase $p$ as the row carries it on arrival (the turns of rule F
already in it). The pointer is kept per Node:
interference is coherent within one Node only, and the offer of a set of
Nodes (a detector, a face) and label $\ell$ is the sum over its Nodes of
$(X_x^2 + Y_x^2)/\mathtt m$, one cell of the ladder, the click landing at the Node
within the set that $u$ selects by the same rungs; all rows at a set share
$\mathtt m$. When the record's live count reaches $0$, with $C_K = \sum R$ over its
offers and the cumulative sums $C_1 \le \dots \le C_K$ (the total) in the declared
order,
\begin{equation}
\rung_k = \left\lfloor \frac{2\Nphi C_k + C_K}{2C_K} \right\rfloor \quad (\rung_0 = 0,\ \rung_K = \Nphi),
\qquad \text{the click is the } k \text{ with } \rung_{k-1} \le u < \rung_k,
\label{eq:rung}
\end{equation}
and the world's list gains one row: the record, $u$, the chosen set and
label, $R$, $C_K$, the count of combinations before and after. For a record
with arms at two rotated sets $A$ and $B$ (settings $a$, $b$) the cells
are the joint outcomes $(o_A, o_B)$ in the arms' declared order, with
the weights
\begin{equation}
R(o_A, o_B) = J(o_A, o_B)^2,\qquad J(o_A, o_B) = \sum_\ell c_\ell\,U_a[o_A][\ell]\,U_b[o_B][\ell],
\label{eq:joint}
\end{equation}
$c_\ell$ the labels' declared weights, $(1, 1)$ in every world here; and
the record is gathered once, when the rows at both arms have ended:
the world's row carries both outcomes at the completion interval, so the
near party's outcome is written at the far party's tick. Reading the
ladder in that order is the same as a coarse rung over $o_A$ followed by
a fine rung over $o_B$ within it with the same $u$, which is how
Theorem~\ref{th:marginals} states it. A row of a gathered record that
reached a set afterwards would offer nothing; no reported world has one,
since completion needs every row ended.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}[One click per record]\label{lem:one}
A record every row of which ends (at a set, a face or the border; not a
row parked at rest or on a periodic axis without a lifetime) completes
and is gathered exactly once: the live count reaches $0$; $\rung_K = \Nphi$
(with $C_K > 0$: for a record born with a nonzero row whose every end
is an offer, the offers summed over all its ends are the born row's
norm up to the tables' rounding, Theorem~\ref{th:isometry}; at $C_K = 0$
the engine gathers nowhere, the auditor's rounds 4 and 4b, record 363
\cite{log}) and $\rung_0 = 0$, so every
$u \in \Z_{\Nphi}$ lies in exactly one cell. Every
record of the worlds below is of this kind. The world's row carries the
completion interval and the last arrival's interval; the delay between
them is a delay of the record, not of the GameBoard.
\end{lemma}

Three things are put in here and not derived, and the paper claims none
of them. Definition~\ref{def:click} \emph{is} the Born rule: the weight is
the squared length of the summed pointer, and the ruler over $u$ is
inverse-transform sampling of a uniform variable, so over births with $u$
uniform on $\Z_{\Nphi}$ the count in channel $k$ is $\rung_k - \rung_{k-1}$, the
difference of two cumulative rungs, Born to the resolution $1/\Nphi$ per cell. No principle
below the square is offered. The uniformity of $u$ is an assumption about
the source: $u = (b - 1) \bmod \Nphi$ is uniform over $\Nphi$ consecutive births,
which is how every run below is made (one birth per $u$), and its
independence of the settings is a fact of a world, not of the model: $u$
is a clock and a chooser is a clock, so they are independent when the
chooser's period is coprime to $\Nphi$ and the run covers their common
period, as in this paper's choosers' world (periods $3$ and $5$ against
$\Nphi = 64$, every bin one birth per $u$, every marginal $32/64$); a chooser
whose period shares a factor with $\Nphi$ (not run) would correlate with $u$
by construction, a correlation of the kind superdeterminism posits. The earlier engine's run \cite{paper1}
is history. $u$ is also the phase every row of
the record starts from, so it enters every table read as a common offset
of the record's phasors: exactly invariant before the tables' rounding
and not after it (the record's total over $u$ takes eight values,
Section~\ref{sec:measure}); the click alone reads $u$ as a number. The
tables at $1/256$ are the third of these three, and a declared input of
the law (Part~I, ``What is put in''). What the paper derives is what
follows from these with the GameBoard's rules: the interference of one record, the exact marginals, the
$\cos^2$ correlation of the pair, GHZ's zeros, and the finite-$\Nphi$ value
of $S$. The click is the only step that removes combinations and adds a
row to the world's list; the birth is the only step that adds
combinations. At the tree of the runs reported here the law was selected by a world
key (\texttt{amplitude}) and the worlds without it read as before; the
archived code has since made the record form the only click and deleted
the key, with dated lines in its register for what its later changes
moved and the integers of series L kept as recorded \cite{beamlaw,register}.

\section{Four theorems}\label{sec:theorems}

\begin{theorem}[The interval is injective between clicks]\label{th:bijection}
For an interval without a click, an end at a face or the border, or a
collision, $M \circ R \circ S \circ F$ is an injective $\Z$-linear map from $\bigoplus_r M_r$ to
$\bigoplus_r M_r$ paired with the apparatus's record of the rows the
splitters and rotators absorbed in that interval; the GameBoard's state
at any interval between two clicks, together with that record,
determines the state at the birth. Without the record the split forgets
the arriving age. A collision, where it acts, is a bijection of the
GameBoard's states that is not linear; none acts in the worlds of this
paper.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
$F$ maps a basis element to a basis element and is injective because the
age is kept whole: $(D, \tau) \mapsto (D, \tau+1)$ is injective and the
Node offset is a function of $(D, \tau)$. $S$ absorbs the arriving row,
whose age, phase and amount are written on the apparatus's click line,
and re-emits at age $0$; given that line it is injective on the
amplitudes by Theorem~\ref{th:isometry}, and without it two rows
differing only in age have the same image: the split is inverted only
because the click line records what it absorbed. $R$ is injective
because $U_{s,t} = U_s\,\mathrm{diag}(1, v(t))$, the turn a bijection and
$U_s^{\mathsf T}U_s = n_s I$. $M$ replaces a
representative by the normal form of the same element of $M_r$, the
identity on the module. A composition of injective linear maps is
injective. The collision permutes the directions of the amount-$1$ rows
at a Node as a function of the whole set present \cite{beamlaw}, a
bijection of states and not a linear map, which is why it is excluded
from the linear statement. The engine's inverse interval is the witness
on worlds without a measured event \cite{beamlaw}; with splitters the
statement is the algebraic one with the record.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[The split is an isometry whose conjugate transpose inverts it]\label{th:isometry}
The split $(w, \mathtt m, p) \mapsto ((wa_i, \mathtt m A, p + t_i))_i$ preserves
$\sum w^2/\mathtt m$ for every nonzero integer vector $(a_i)$, and the conjugate
transpose of the table (the same weights with the turns reversed)
applied to its outputs, followed by the normal form, returns the input up
to the scaling $(kw, k^2\mathtt m)$ with $k = A$. The rotation satisfies
$U_s^{\mathsf T} U_s = n_s I$ exactly with $n_s = C'[s]^2 + S'[s]^2$, and
multiplies a record's norm by $n_s/65536$: an isometry only up to the
tables' rounding ($65705/65536$ at $\Nphi = 64$, $s = 1$).
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
$\sum_i (wa_i)^2/(\mathtt m A) = w^2 \sum_i a_i^2/(\mathtt m A) = w^2/\mathtt m$. The conjugate
transpose maps the outputs $(wa_i, \mathtt m A, p + t_i)$ to $\sum_i a_i (w a_i)$
at the phase $p$ on the input direction with multiplicity $\mathtt m A \cdot A$,
that is $(Aw, A^2 \mathtt m, p) \sim (w, \mathtt m, p)$; for two inputs at one splitter
the cross terms carry phases differing by $\Nphi/2$ (a reflection's quarter
turn taken forward and its reverse taken back) and cancel in the normal
form, which the check with the pairs $(20, 21)$, $(3, 4, 5)$ and
$(119, 120, 169)$ confirms integer by integer \cite{design} (with the
turns kept instead of reversed the inputs are not returned), and the
engine's merge reproduces on its own rows \cite{beamlaw}. For $U_s$ the
diagonal of $U_s^{\mathsf T}U_s$ is $C'^2 + S'^2$ and the off-diagonal
$C'S' - S'C' = 0$ in integers.
\end{proof}

So a record's norm is conserved exactly by $S$ and by the flight, and by
$R$ and by every read through the tables only within the tables' rounding
($-351/65536$ to $+361/65536$ per row for the powers of two through
$65536$); the integer
Mach-Zehnder's total over the 64 birth phases takes eight values from
$65448/65536$ to $65773/65536$, the same eight on every one of its ten
worlds \cite{register}. The normalisation by $C_K$ in \eqref{eq:rung} makes
the click's probabilities sum to one at completion; it does not restore
the norm along the way, and the paper claims exact unitarity nowhere.

\begin{theorem}[Exact marginals; no-signalling]\label{th:marginals}
For a pair with labels $\{0, 1\}$ of equal weight and settings $a, b$, let
$J(o_A, o_B) = \sum_\ell U_a[o_A][\ell]\,U_b[o_B][\ell]$ and
$R(o_A, o_B) = J^2$. Then the first party's outcome is $+$ exactly for
$u < \Nphi/2$ and $-$ for $u \ge \Nphi/2$, for every $(a, b)$ and every $\Nphi$; and
the second party's count of $+$ is exactly $\Nphi/2$ for every $(a, b)$ unless
$2\Nphi R(+,+)/C_K$ is an odd integer (a tie of the rung), in which case it is
$\Nphi/2 + 1$. No tie occurs for any setting pair at $\Nphi = 8, 16, 24, 32, 48,
64, 96, 128, 192, 256, 512, 1024$, nor at the CHSH labels for any
multiple of $8$ up to $4096$ (computed from the rule, \cite{checks}; the
gate review found none at $64$, $256$, $1024$ and at $4096$ for
$a = 0, 1024$, \cite{log}); the
reviewer also recomputed the marginals from the rows of one registered
world at $\Nphi = 64$ for all 4096 setting pairs, $32/64$ in every pair
\cite{log}.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Write $n_a = C'[a]^2 + S'[a]^2$. Since the rows of $U_b$ are orthogonal on
the integers, $\sum_{o_B} J(o_A, o_B)^2 = n_a n_b$ for each $o_A$, so
$C_K = 2n_a n_b$ and the coarse cumulative after the first party's $+$ is
$C_K/2$: $\rung = \lfloor (\Nphi C_K + C_K)/(2C_K)\rfloor = \lfloor (\Nphi+1)/2 \rfloor =
\Nphi/2$. For the second party, $R(o_A, o_B) = R(-o_A, -o_B)$ (the rows of
$U_s$ are each other's quarter turn), so with $x = \Nphi R(+,+)/C_K$ the counts
are $c_{++} = \lfloor x + \tfrac12\rfloor$ and $c_{-+} = \rung_3 - \Nphi/2$ with
$\rung_3 = \lfloor (2\Nphi(C_K - R(+,+)) + C_K)/(2C_K)\rfloor = \Nphi + \lfloor \tfrac12 -
x\rfloor$. Hence $c_{++} + c_{-+} = \Nphi/2 + \lfloor y\rfloor + \lfloor 1 -
y\rfloor$ with $y = x + \tfrac12$, which is $\Nphi/2$ when $y \notin \Z$ and
$\Nphi/2 + 1$ when $y \in \Z$. The tie condition is $2x \in 2\Z + 1$, that
is $2\Nphi R(+,+)/C_K$ odd. The absence of ties at the listed $\Nphi$ is the
computation over every setting pair \cite{checks}. Which party is first
is the world file's declaration of the arms' order, not a property of the GameBoard: the model has a preferred party by declaration, and
swapping the arms moves outcomes between the parties while the counts
stay \cite{log}.
\end{proof}

The strict-crossing rung $\Nphi C_k > uC_K$ gives the second party $33/64$ in
$3944$ of the $4096$ setting pairs at $\Nphi = 64$, a signalling of $1/\Nphi$ made
by the rounding \cite{design}; the nearest rung of \eqref{eq:rung} is what
Theorem~\ref{th:marginals} needs. The theorem's scope is what it states:
a pair of two labels with equal weights, the CHSH form, and the rung of
\eqref{eq:rung}; unequal weights and more than two labels are not covered,
and no-signalling for them is open. Where the two settings meet is
explicit in Definition~\ref{def:click}: $B$'s rung is computed from
$R(o_A, o_B)$ with $a$ and $o_A$ supplied by the layer. That the marginal
at $B$ is nevertheless $\Nphi/2$ for every $a$ is the content of the theorem;
the joint counts are not local, and the model does not claim they are.

\begin{theorem}[The finite-$\Nphi$ Bell value]\label{th:bell}
With the counts of Theorem~\ref{th:marginals} and off a tie,
$E_{\Nphi}(a, b) = 4c_{++}/\Nphi - 1$ and
\begin{equation}
\bigl|E_{\Nphi}(a, b) - \cos(2\pi(a-b)/\Nphi)\bigr| \le \frac{2}{\Nphi} + 4\arcsin\frac{\sqrt2}{2\varrho} < \frac{2}{\Nphi} + 0.0111,\qquad \varrho = N_t - \tfrac{\sqrt2}{2},
\label{eq:ebound}
\end{equation}
so at the labels $(0, \Nphi/8, \Nphi/4, 3\Nphi/8)$, $|S(\Nphi) - 2\sqrt2| \le 8/\Nphi + 0.0444$.
$S(\Nphi)$ is an exact rational for each $\Nphi$; it lies above $2\sqrt2$ for
$252$ of the $512$ multiples of $8$ up to $4096$ (among them $\Nphi = 16, 32$
with $S = 3$ and $\Nphi = 128$ with $S = 23/8$) and equals $181/64 =
2.828125$ at every power of two from $512$ through $4096$ \cite{checks}.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
$c_{++} = \lfloor x + \tfrac12\rfloor$ with $x = \Nphi R(+,+)/C_K$ gives
$|c_{++} - x| \le \tfrac12$ and $E_{\Nphi} = 4c_{++}/\Nphi - 1$ from the mirror
symmetry, so $|E_{\Nphi} - (4R(+,+)/C_K - 1)| \le 2/\Nphi$. The vector
$(C'[s], S'[s])$ differs from $256(\cos\theta_s, \sin\theta_s)$,
$\theta_s = \pi s/\Nphi$, by at most $\tfrac12$ per component, so its
direction differs from $\theta_s$ by at most $\delta = \arcsin(\sqrt2/(2\varrho))$
with $\varrho = N_t - \sqrt2/2 = 255.29$, below every table vector's length
(each component within $\tfrac12$ of $256\cos$ or $256\sin$), so
$4\delta = 0.01108 < 0.0111$ and $16\delta = 0.04432 < 0.0444$;
$J(+,+)/\sqrt{n_a n_b}$ is the cosine of the angle between the two
directions, $\cos(\theta_a - \theta_b + \epsilon)$ with $|\epsilon| \le
2\delta$, and $4R(+,+)/C_K - 1 = 2\cos^2(\cdot) - 1 = \cos(2(\theta_a -
\theta_b) + 2\epsilon)$, within $4\delta$ of $\cos(2\pi(a-b)/\Nphi)$. The
four settings add the bounds. The values of $S(\Nphi)$ are the computation
from these formulas with the repository's tables \cite{checks}, which
reproduces the design's $176/64$, $720/256$ and $2896/1024$ at $\Nphi = 64,
256, 1024$ and the engine's three values below.
\end{proof}

What Theorem~\ref{th:bell} does not say: that $S(\Nphi) \le 2\sqrt2$, which is
false for about half the $\Nphi$; nor that the deficit is $O(1/\Nphi)$ from below,
which holds at the three $\Nphi$ the engine ran and not in general. The
tables' term in \eqref{eq:ebound} does not shrink with $\Nphi$; it is the
resolution $1/256$ of the circle's tables. At the CHSH labels the tables'
entries are the same at every $\Nphi$ ($237, 98$ and $181, 181$), so
$E(0, \Nphi/8) = 46565/65773$ and $E(\Nphi/4, \Nphi/8) = 46452/65773$ exactly at every
$\Nphi$ before the rounding of the cells; the rounding gives $181/64$ at
the powers of two from $512$ through $8192$ and $5793/2048 = 2.828613$
at $16384$ and $32768$, where the second pair of settings rounds to
$2893/4096$ (\cite{checks}, \texttt{s\_powers\_of\_two}; the
tables' bound $2\Nphi \le 65536$ stops the computation there); with the
tables fixed the limit in $\Nphi$ is $2(46565 + 46452)/65773 =
186034/65773 = 2.828425$, and $2\sqrt2$ is the joint limit in $\Nphi$
and $N_t$ alone \cite{checks,register}. At a tie $c_{--} = c_{++} - 1$ and
$E_{\Nphi}$ drops by $2/\Nphi$; no tie occurs at the CHSH labels up to $4096$.

\section{The measurements}\label{sec:measure}

Every run of this section is recorded in the experiments register of the
archived code as series L, with its world file, its expectation written
before the run and the source fingerprint \texttt{ff5c382d672f}
\cite{register}; the gate worlds (CNOT, the ceiling) were run again after
the gate's review fixed its bookkeeping, the register recording the same
integers. The figures were drawn from the 46 worlds of series L run
again on the paper's tree (the 45 under the key, whose integers the
summary carries; \texttt{slits\_one}, the reference world without the
key, read by the generator) (the merge \texttt{4ccf65c7} of the main branch's
\texttt{d2064195}, the fingerprint \texttt{731d0f56c9f9} of \texttt{figures/summary.json}), every integer
equal to the register's, the gate worlds included \cite{checks}; the two
worlds of L7 (the cone) were run later, on the tree that made the record
form the only click (its head \texttt{725d811f} and the main branch's
\texttt{0f193fdb}, fingerprint \texttt{4bf55a62e6fd}) \cite{register}. Each
world is run for its first $\Nphi$ births, one per $u = 0, \dots, \Nphi-1$ (per
setting bin in the choosers' world), and the
reading tool of the archive replays each run's register through the
layer and equals the run's world list on every run; the tool builds the
same layer the engine built, so this is a consistency check of the log
against the run, not an independent implementation. Every number below
is a count of clicks (a detector reading); the rows' bookkeeping is the
GameBoard's and is reported where it explains a count. One expectation
was re-pinned once after a first run: the two slits, from $31/14/19$ to
$34/15/15$, when the per-Node coherence rule was decided at the review of
the implementation's third stage; every other row's expectation stood
from before its first run \cite{register,log}.

Three departures from the design's expectations were recorded and not
moved. The record's total over $u$ is not one number but eight, from
$65448/65536$ to $65773/65536$, the tables' rounding depending on $u$;
the rule normalises by the total at completion, so no click moved. The
two-slit world's shares are this geometry's (the wall freed within six
Nodes of each opening so that every fan row leaves the plane; the faces a
third destination) and not the design's $3/5$ and $2/5$; every set's
count equals the reading's. The design's bound $|E - \cos| \le 1/\Nphi$ holds
at $\Nphi = 1024$ and fails at $64$ and $4096$, as Theorem~\ref{th:bell}
allows: the cells' grain at $64$, the tables' $1/256$ at $4096$.

\section{The Bell value at finite $\Nphi$ and the experiments}\label{sec:bell}

$S(\Nphi)$ is a property of the model that nothing in the model fixes: $\Nphi$ is
a declared width of the world. The model gives a prediction only once $\Nphi$
is chosen, and what a measurement of nature does is constrain $\Nphi$, under
three assumptions the paper states and does not defend: that nature's
pairs are this model's pairs at some $\Nphi$, that the experiment's
fair-sampling assumption holds, and that the experiment's four settings
are the model's labels $(0, \Nphi/8, \Nphi/4, 3\Nphi/8)$. The third matters because
$E$ depends on $(a, b)$ beyond $a - b$: at $\Nphi = 256$ the maximum of $S$
over all setting quadruples is $91/32 = 2.84375$ and $181{,}152$
quadruples lie within three standard deviations of the measured value,
while at $\Nphi = 64$ the maximum over all quadruples is $11/4$, so that
exclusion stands whatever the settings \cite{checks}. Under the three,
the photon-pair value $S = 2.82759 \pm 0.00051$ \cite{poh2015} is
incompatible with $\Nphi = 64$ (152 standard deviations) and $\Nphi = 256$ ($30$ by the paper's check, $31$ by the derivation's 24.4),
compatible with the powers of two from 512 through 8192 (one standard deviation
above), admits its first $\Nphi$ at 184, and excludes 136 of the 512 values
up to 4096 at five standard deviations, the largest 3016 \cite{checks};
the archived engine declares $\Nphi$ a power of two, so the other multiples
of $8$ are the formula's, not runnable worlds. Of the loophole-free
experiments, \cite{hensen2015} reports a CHSH value, $S = 2.42 \pm
0.20$, which excludes no $\Nphi$ at three standard deviations;
\cite{giustina2015,shalm2015} report a CH-Eberhard quantity whose
comparison needs the model's detector efficiency, which it lacks
(Section~\ref{sec:notclaimed}). A
no-signalling value above $2\sqrt2$ is not forbidden by no-signalling
\cite{pr1994}; at $\Nphi = 16$ and $32$ the model gives $S = 3$ with the
marginals exactly $1/2$. Agreement of one value with one experiment
validates nothing beyond that; a comparison with noise and detector
inefficiency in the model's own terms has not been made, and the model
has no rule that would give it.

\section{The click in the causal anatomy of Bell's theorem}\label{sec:causal}

Write $\lambda$ for what is fixed before the settings, $a, b$ for the
settings, $A, B$ for the outcomes. In the terms of \cite{jarrett1984,shimony1986}
as \cite{paper1} used them: $\lambda$ is $u$ with the record, chosen at
the birth before any setting exists and read by no rule that sets a
setting (measurement independence holds in the registered choosers'
world by its coprime periods, Section~\ref{sec:model}, not by
construction); the joint
outcome is one function of $(u, a, b)$, computed once at completion;
$A$, the first party in the declared order of the arms, is $+$ for
$u < \Nphi/2$, independent of both settings (parameter independence holds
at $A$, and $A$'s marginal is $1/2$); $B$ is a function of $(u, a, b)$:
parameter dependence at $B$, exactly where the bonded pair of
\cite{paper1} had it, and outcome independence in the trivial sense that
$B$ is deterministic given $\lambda$ and both settings. Which party is
$A$ is the world file's declaration, a preferred party by declaration
and not by the GameBoard. The model is
deterministic and parameter-dependent, and measurement-independent in the
registered choosers' world, the class
Bell's theorem requires of a deterministic model above the bound
\cite{bell1964,chsh1969}; Theorem~\ref{th:marginals} is what keeps the
dependence from being a signal, as \cite{grw1980} showed of quantum
mechanics. What is new against \cite{paper1} is where the dependence
lives: not in a configured cosine of the two settings held by a registry,
but in one gather reading one record's rows at both arms, whose products
of the two rotations' entries give $\cos^2$ by the same arithmetic that
gives one record's fringe. The law is the tables' and the rule's; it was
not written into the pair.

\section{What is new}\label{sec:new}

\begin{itemize}
\item One measurement rule, exact in integers, from which interference,
the pair's correlation with exact marginals, GHZ and the gate follow on one GameBoard without a configured correlation law, with the rule's
non-locality stated as one gather of one record from both settings.
\item Four theorems about that rule, with the tie, the tables' rounding
and the apparatus's record in their statements, and one exact table
$S(\Nphi)$ that puts the model on both sides of Tsirelson's bound.
\item Runs recorded before the text, the first $\Nphi$ births one per $u$, replayed
from their own registers by the layer alone, and the departures from
the design's expectations kept as measured.
\item A transport of light on $\Z^3$ isotropic within $1/T_D$ and free
of dispersion in every direction while its carried phase interferes,
which no local linear wave scheme on the GameBoard can be
(Remark~\ref{rem:nodispersion}); with its price stated: no wave on the GameBoard, and a photon's energy entering only by an assumed reading.
\end{itemize}

\section{What is not claimed}\label{sec:notclaimed}

\begin{itemize}
\item Not a derivation of the Born rule's constants or of the uniform
birth phase: the form of the square is forced under the hypotheses of
Section~\ref{sec:gleason}; its harmonic constants and the birth phase's
uniformity are not, and are the rule (Section~\ref{sec:model}).
\item Not a local model of the pair: the record is gathered once from
both settings, and the near party's row is written at the far party's
tick; no-signalling is proved for the equal-weight pair and open beyond
it; the first party is the world file's choice.
\item Not exact unitarity on the GameBoard: the tables' norm is within
$-351$ to $+361$ of $65536$ per row for the powers of two through
$65536$, the rotation is an isometry only up
to that, and the record's total over $u$ takes eight values.
\item Not a fixed $\Nphi$, hence not a prediction of nature's $S$ at
every $\Nphi$: at the powers of two from $512$ through $8192$ it is
$181/64$ and at $16384$ and $32768$ $5793/2048$ (Section~\ref{sec:differs});
elsewhere a constraint on $\Nphi$ under stated assumptions.
\item Not a new number for $c$, and not $c$ from two rules: $1/\sqrt3$
Links per interval is the Courant bound of the cubic lattice, reached by
the same argument, and the lattice Boltzmann sound speed; the table's
sitting at the supremum is the declared wall $T_D$, a rule chosen among
few (P9), and the value given it the flight operator's norm (the
derivation's 24.1, row 9).
Proposition~\ref{prop:pace} adds the exact integer table at the bound and
the grain's anisotropy as a bound on $N_l$.
\item Not a continuum limit of the click chapter's model as a whole
(Section~\ref{sec:limits} states its computed limits;
Table~\ref{tab:consequences} the law's), not spin or polarisation (the labels are
abstract), not bosons or fermions, not Lorentz invariance (one speed for
every direction, no boost; Section~\ref{sec:lorentz}), not the classical limit as click density
(proposed, not run), not a treatment of noise or detector inefficiency.
\item Not the tree of the archive's latest version: the runs are the
tree named in Section~\ref{sec:measure}; the later code made the record
form the only click, put $u$ on the row as the record's own field (read
by no rule of the GameBoard) and re-pinned the ticks of every lamp world.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Two questions of the click, open.} The law as stated in
Part~I has two kinds of reading. A body reads the rows at its Node by
bilinear forms (the push, the owed count, the moments) and deletes no
record: a paid row it absorbs goes to the books' absorbed line, its label
into the reader. The click reads a completed record at a detector
declared in the world file, keeps one cell and deletes the record. Which
physical systems are detectors in that sense, whether a star clicks or
only an apparatus does, is not a question the law answers; it is open,
with nothing claimed. And the click's place in time admits a reading,
the program's, stated here as a hypothesis: between clicks the linear
block is injective given the apparatus's record
(Theorem~\ref{th:bijection}) and every path of a record exists at once;
the click keeps one cell, and the state's future is that cell's, so the
click chooses one path among all. The only computed quantity near that
reading is the entropy of the derivation's section 14, the base-two
logarithm of the count of torus points consistent with the click list,
produced only at the click, the cancel and the leaks, with the identity
that the bits read plus the bits not read of $u$ are $\log_2 \Nphi$ per record (the outcome $K$ the observation, $u$ kept in the world's list as a diagnostic),
checked on six registered worlds \cite{derivations}; the quantity is
computed, the reading is not derived from it, and what would prove or
refute the reading is not defined here.

\section{The hypotheses of the program}\label{sec:hyp}

Everything the program stated beside the click chapter's result at the
time of its runs is listed here with its status then and claimed
nowhere; where an item has since become a row of
Table~\ref{tab:consequences}, the table's verdict is the paper's and
this paragraph is the chapter's history. ``Follows from'' is written only
where the rule it follows from is named; the rest is conjectured within
the same GameBoard and not a consequence of the click. Status (the Introduction's labels in the register's words): registered (measured)
(a run in the archived register with a fingerprint and the verdict
quoted), design (a check script's integers, no run: a computation), not run (open), published (cited).

\paragraph{Consequences of the click model.} Entanglement without
maintenance and its end at any intermediate click (from ``nothing between
the birth and the click removes a combination''; registered: the pair
unchanged with one party 116 Links farther, the correlation gone with a
which-path read). The classical limit as click density (from the click
removing combinations and the birth adding them: a body clicked at every
interval has one path; principle of the program; not run). Delayed
choice and the quantum eraser (from the ruler being read at the record's
completion; not run). Hong-Ou-Mandel (would need rows of two records to
merge and cancel at one Node, a rule the model does not have: the merge
is within one record; not run). The quantum computer in principle and its two ceilings (from
the gate and the register: $n \times 2^n$ rows per path after $n$
entangling gates; on one path 62 balanced splits, 13 passes of a
$(3, 4, 5)$ splitter, 6 of $(20, 21, 29)$, 3 label rotations fit within
$2^{62}$ and a fourth rotation is refused at load; Grover's six rotations
are exact only on the host's integers; design and registered as far as
the refusal). If the GameBoard is a finite machine this is a hard ceiling
on an entangled register, at a width and depth fixed by the store's
capacity and the register's width and not by any law; nature shows no
such ceiling at the sizes reached, and the model fixes no number until a
capacity is declared a law. The bits a click carries: at most $\log_2 \Nphi$
per click, since $u$ takes $\Nphi$ values and a cell needs $C_K/2\Nphi$ of the
weight; zero at a distance (Theorem~\ref{th:marginals}); derived from the
ruler, not run against an experiment. The boundary between classical and
quantum is the click: sharp per record (a distinguishing click removes
every other combination at once) and continuous over births by the
density of clicks; a partial which-path device is a different apparatus
and a different world in this model: a phase window admitting part of
the circle on one arm of the Mach-Zehnder, a rule outside
Definition~\ref{def:rules} that reads the row's phase and so $u$,
computed from the rule \cite{checks} on the model as defined here, where
the row's phase starts at $u$ (the archived code's window reads the path
phase, phase less $u$, and this computation does not describe it), gives
neither the linear nor the quadratic duality
relation between visibility and path knowledge, and an absorber count
that depends on the arm phase and on the window's centre, because the
window and the ruler read the same $u$; this is an open problem of the
rule, reported and not a prediction.

\paragraph{Conjectured within the same GameBoard, not consequences of the
click.} The forces as columns of one coupling per unit of content
(registered: Newton's identities, Gauss's law, the third law hold on the
plane exactly; the far field of a six-beam source follows the Node
count); the nucleus (registered: the deuteron bound at one Link, the
strong reading a cut $1/r^2$ with the opposite sign and no Yukawa tail,
the alpha a line and not a square); the weak force (registered: the
neutrino's admitted fraction $w/\Nphi$ exactly and flat in energy, the
neutron's decay a step with a line spectrum, a bound neutron later or
never: plain disagreements with nature, kept as the law's limits); light
beside a mass (registered: neither bent nor delayed without a rule that
reads the crowd; under the meeting bent toward the mass with the sign and
the $M/b$ form and a grain $10^4$ times nature's angle, not delayed); the
clock's redshift in a field (registered: the $1/r$ form, a pinned
criterion missed at three radii); the Hubble diagram behind the detector
(registered: linear near, coasting far, nothing accelerates); Bohr's
lines (registered: no orbit closes under whole kicks, the lines not
read); the redshift from delay growth on a closed GameBoard and the
supernova test (published, \cite{paper2}); the masses and charges of the
families as the universe's initialisation, not derivable in this law
(decided on the program's derivation; the binding energy that costs
content an open design); dark matter as a closed dimension, $G$ from the GameBoard's width, a moving body's clock (not run). Each is stated in the
archived register in the words of its verdict, and none is cited by the
result of this chapter. One hypothesis is later than the register and is
named here once: massive-rows-v1, a massive quantum in flight as a record
of rows and matter as a body after the click, the route to
Schr\"odinger's equation, decided by the owner for its derivation first
and derived in part (records 331, 332 and 336 \cite{log}; the
derivation's 23; Section~\ref{sec:qm}); its design ordered and not
delivered (record 336), not built, its pin written, not run.

\section{Where the law differs, by how much, and what bounds it}\label{sec:differs}

A theory is tested where it differs. The derivation's section 24
\cite{derivations} lists every computable difference between the law
as declared and quantum mechanics or relativity, with the law's number,
the theory's, the size, the experiment that bounds it today and the
verdict on main (Table~\ref{tab:differs}); where a hypothesis beside
the law (the covariant readings of Section~\ref{sec:lorentz},
optical-v1) changes a row, the row says so, and the verdict stays the
law's as built. Twelve of the register's twenty-two readings fail and one more under the presence word of the clock, the alternative to
the assumed age word
(Section~\ref{sec:nature}); this table is what those failures look
like once each difference is stated as a number a measurement can
refute, and it names the one difference that is open and within reach.

\paragraph{The one prediction the paper can carry.} Of the open rows
within reach the smallest is row 2: for $\Nphi$ a power of two at or above
$512$ the law's CHSH sum on the maximally entangled pair at the CHSH
settings is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:prediction}
S_{\mathrm{U24}} = \frac{181}{64} = 2.828125 \ \text{exactly}, \qquad
\Delta S = S_{\mathrm{U24}} - 2\sqrt2 = -3.02 \times 10^{-4},
\end{equation}
with the marginals exactly $1/2$ (no signalling, Theorem~\ref{th:marginals}),
unchanged by the tables' scale from $256$ to $2^{20}$ and by $\Nphi$ from
$512$ to $4096$ (the registered $2896/1024$, $11584/4096$ and, from
this run, $1448/512$, all $181/64$; record 102 \cite{log} for the powers of two from $512$
through $4096$, the check \texttt{s\_powers\_of\_two} \cite{checks}
through $32768$: $181/64$ holds through $8192$ and not beyond, the rule
giving $5793/2048$ at $16384$ and $32768$, so the prediction is the
exact function of $\Nphi$ and $N_t$ and this row is its value on the
registered range): a
prediction computed in advance; the correlations at the two pairs of
settings differ, $725/1024$ and $723/1024$ at $\Nphi = 4096$, above
and below $1/\sqrt2$ by $9.0 \times 10^{-4}$ and $1.05 \times 10^{-3}$,
their mean $7.6 \times 10^{-5}$ below it. What bounds it today: \cite{poh2015} measured $S = 2.82759 \pm
0.00051$, a deficit $0.00084 \pm 0.00051$ from $2\sqrt2$; the law's
deficit $0.00030$ is inside at $1.1$ standard errors, and the same
measurement excludes the law's $\Nphi = 256$ (row 1) and, under the
comparison's identification, $136$ of the $512$ multiples of $8$ of
row 3 (Section~\ref{sec:bell}: $\Nphi = 184$ and others survive it);
the engine's power of two is a declaration, not an experimental
exclusion. What refutes it: a CHSH measurement on a
maximally entangled pair with an uncertainty below $1 \times 10^{-4}$
reading a deficit below $2 \times 10^{-4}$ ($S$ above $2.8282$), or
any deficit above $4 \times 10^{-4}$ outside its error; an uncertainty
five times smaller than that experiment's, below $1 \times 10^{-4}$,
decides, with the settings and the detection model stated
(\cite{poh2015} optimized its settings rather than the canonical
labels and discusses setting-dependent detection; the comparison
assumes the identification of Section~\ref{sec:bell}). What the law owes before the pin
is final: the Bell worlds at $\Nphi = 512$ (written by the generator for
this run) and the registered $\Nphi = 4096$ worlds run under the one click and the birth
wheel on the registered geometry (the values above are the design's
host map at $\Nphi = 64$, $256$ and $1024$, the registered runs of
2026-09-20 at $\Nphi = 1024$ and $4096$ before the wheel, series L6's
$2896/1024$ and $11584/4096$, and record 102's computation for the
powers of two through $4096$); the pin before that run,
$11584/4096$ at every quadruple of the CHSH settings, the marginals
$2048/4096$, the counts over $N_u$ births within one of $N_u$ times their
rungs. The run was made (2026-09-21; the register's block ``Bell at $\Nphi =
512$ and $4096$ under the click and the wheel, the pin of 24.4''
\cite{register}): on the eight worlds at $\Nphi = 512$ and $4096$ under the
one click and the birth wheel, every pin met, $S = 1448/512$ and
$11584/4096$, both $181/64$ exactly, the marginals $256/512$ and
$2048/4096$ on every world, every count equal to its pinned count; PASS,
no number moved; the test \texttt{test\_amplitude\_bell\_24\_4} replays
the worlds bit-exact. The prediction
stands pinned, run and re-run by the second runner, who read $S \times
\Nphi = 1448$ and $11584$ again from the register's worlds and checked
them from the four directions (\cite{replications}, the block of 24.4:
REPLICATED; record 353 \cite{log}); what remains is nature's measurement at five times the
precision of \cite{poh2015}. Why this one: rows 5 to 9 and 13 to 19
are refuted on main or below reach; row 4 is a grain the owner has
declared an input (the tables' scale, record 328) and falls with it,
not with the law; row 11 is zero; row 20 is a rounding. Row 2 is a
number of the law's own structure, the rungs of the ladder at a
power-of-two $\Nphi$ and not the tables, the same at every $\Nphi$ the
register uses and a different number at $16384$, and one experiment
already sits at its edge: a CHSH sum three parts in ten thousand below
Tsirelson's bound, exactly $181/64$ on the registered range.

\part{What returns, what does not, and the decision on Lorentz}\label{part:limits}

\section{Continuum limits: what returns and what does not}\label{sec:limits}

A model in integers is judged also by what it returns as its resolution
grows, as a Fourier sum returns $\pi$. Two limits follow from the
definitions and are computed in \cite{checks} (Tsirelson in
\texttt{limits.txt}, the cone in \texttt{information\_transfer.txt}), a
third (Young) is partial, two conservations hold exactly, and two limits
do not follow and are stated as such.

\paragraph{Tsirelson's value as a limit.} The bound of
Theorem~\ref{th:bell} has two terms, $2/\Nphi$ from the ladder's rungs and
$4\arcsin(\sqrt2/(2(N_t - \sqrt2/2)))$ from the tables' scale $N_t$ ($256$ in
every run of series L). As $\Nphi$ and $N_t$ grow both vanish: $S(\Nphi, N_t) \to 2\sqrt2$. The
computed values are $S = 2.75$, $2.8125$ and $2.828125$ at $\Nphi = 64$, $256$
and $1024$, unchanged by $N_t$ from $256$ to $2^{20}$ at these $\Nphi$ (the rungs
decide), with the bound $0.125$, $0.031$ and $0.0078$ at $N_t = 2^{20}$
($0.169$, $0.076$ and $0.052$ at $N_t = 256$). The
finite-$\Nphi$ departures of Section~\ref{sec:bell} are the terms of this
limit, not a second law.

\paragraph{The light cone and the wavelength.} The flight table moves
every direction at $1/\sqrt3$ Links per interval within $1/T_D$
(Proposition~\ref{prop:pace}): $0.8$
percent fast on an axis, $0.5$ on the plane diagonal, exact on the body
diagonal (the register's L7: a row of $17$ Links on an axis and a row of
$24$ Links on the plane diagonal, at the same Euclidean distance to one
percent, reach their counters at the same age $29$). That anisotropy is
set by the flight scale $N_l = 64$ and does not shrink with the Link or
the age; every declared direction's speed reaches $1/\sqrt3$ only as $N_l$
grows, a second parameter beside the tables' $N_t$ (the front's shape is
the fan's, a third), and then $c = a/(\Delta t\sqrt3)$, $\Delta t$ the
interval's length. A family whose
phase turns $n/d$ steps per interval gives two paths of one record a
phase difference of $n/d$ times their difference in age, so a record's
interference wavelength is $c\Nphi d/n$ Links along the flight; a plane wave
of a stream of records is not computed here (a stream's phase at a fixed
interval depends on the lamp's rate as much as on the family's turn).

\paragraph{Young's fringes and the fan.} The two-slit world of L2
correlates $0.368$ with the cosine pattern and $0.744$ with the incoherent
sum, and its clicks fall on $14$ pixels. Three things hide the fringes,
in this order \cite{log}. First the wheel: $u = (b-1) \bmod 64$ repeats
with the births, so the $19$ cells landed by the first $64$ births are the
cells of every birth after them and no number of births adds a pixel to
Figure~\ref{fig:slits}; a map of the program's design that reproduces the register's $64$
clicks exactly shows it, and proposes a wheel of $2^{12}$ births beside
$\Nphi$. Second the fan: each opening re-emits along $91$ directions, and only
$27$ of the $75$ pixels reached receive rows from both openings; where one
opening alone reaches a pixel there is nothing to interfere. A computation
with $K$ directions per opening spaced equally in angle (the angular
measure) gives the correlation with the cosine $0.45$ at $K = 91$ (the
register's $0.37$ in its own geometry), $0.84$ at $361$ and $0.90$ from
$721$ on, every pixel seeing both openings from $721$; the GameBoard's own
fan of primitive directions with equal weights has not converged by the
width $64$ ($0.38$, $0.57$, $0.65$ at the widths $12$, $32$, $64$, a comb) and does with each direction weighted by the
angle it covers ($0.90$) \cite{log}. Third the flight's rounding to whole
intervals: $0.07$ in an idealised two-path computation at this
wavelength, $0.02$ on the world itself. The limit of every direction with
its angular weight returns Young's pattern up to a plateau at $0.90$ (a
plateau whose cause is not computed here); the same map predicts, for a
wheel, the screen's fan (one direction per pixel from each opening, equal
multiplicity) and the exact phase, fringes at the Euclidean spacing with
the counts' correlation with the two-source cosine about $0.96$ over
$4096$ births, a prediction pinned before its run; for the angular fan
alone it pins fringes in the weights and none in the clicks until the
wheel \cite{log}. The run under the wheel and the exact phase was made
after this chapter's runs and is registered (L2b, $4096$ births): the
dark pixels $0$ to $3$ as pinned; the counts' correlation with the
cosine $0.891$ against the pinned $0.96$, the pin being the screen's
fan's and not this world's Farey fan, as the register says; the
visibility $0.966$ against the weights' $0.954$; every cell's count
within $2$ of its rung where the pin said within one, the rungs being
per record and the tables' eight totals moving a rung by one more; the
wheel turning the weights into counts; the bright pixels $19$ to $51$,
the map's $28$ to $29$ being the screen's fan's
(Table~\ref{tab:consequences}). The chapter's own runs stand as
recorded.

\paragraph{Conservation, not yet a continuity equation.} Two things are
conserved exactly on the GameBoard: the content (measured, in transit and
escaped sum to a constant at every interval, the books) and, through
every split, $\sum w^2/\mathtt m$ (Theorem~\ref{th:isometry}). The offered norm
is not: the tables' rounding moves a record's total over eight values
(Section~\ref{sec:measure}), and where paths of one record meet at one
Node their cross terms enter, the two-slit record offering $847181/745472
= 1.136$ of its birth norm. A current on the Links is not defined in this
paper, so $\partial_t \rho + \nabla \cdot j = 0$ is the derivation's task,
not a result here; what the limit inherits is the two exact conservations.

\paragraph{What does not return, and why.} The GameBoard and its interval
are a rest frame. The front is isotropic to $0.8$ percent in that frame; nothing in the
definitions says that a moving body reads the same $c$, so Lorentz
invariance is not a limit of this model but a property it must show or
lack, on worlds with moving clocks (the program's, not the click chapter's;
Section~\ref{sec:lorentz} states what the program's derivation has found
and the decision taken). The
field equations of gravitation are not reachable from a model without
mass: the program's rule that held content delays the flight would give,
in the limit, an optical metric and its geodesics, not the equation the
delay field obeys; that derivation is open and listed in
Section~\ref{sec:hyp}. The paper claims the two limits, the partial third
up to its plateau, and the two conservations above, and neither of these
two.

\section{Lorentz: what the law predicts, and the decision taken}\label{sec:lorentz}

The worlds of the click chapter have no body and no clock in motion, so nothing
in Sections~\ref{sec:model} to \ref{sec:limits} depends on this section;
it is here because a model with one pace in a rest frame owes the reader
its position on Lorentz invariance. The position, the author's decision
of 2026-09-21 \cite{log}, is that the law as declared does not give
Lorentz's factor and says so as a falsifiable prediction, and that a
hypothesis is built beside it for comparison: first the seventh
operation, an integer root at a declared grain slowing a body's counter
and contracting its bond, and then, once the derivation had named
exactly where the factor is and is not in the law (a theorem of its
linear block), the covariant readings within the six operations in its
place \cite{log}. The
statements below are the program's derivation on the same GameBoard with
its law of bodies (a body's drive; the push of a body by the rows it
reads), made on paper and in host scripts from that law's definitions,
not run \cite{derivations}; each carries its section there. Notation:
$\beta$ (beta) is a body's speed as a fraction of $c$, $\gamma$ (gamma)
$= 1/\sqrt{1 - \beta^2}$ the Lorentz factor, $\mathbf n$ the unit vector
from the retarded position of a source, $\boldsymbol\beta$ the velocity
vector in units of $c$.

\paragraph{The theorem of covariant readings.} The linear block's
continuum limit is the retarded scalar wave equation at $c$ (asserted
in the derivation, not among this paper's computed limits; the delay
field of Table~\ref{tab:consequences}), and the symmetry of that limit
is the Poincar\'e group: every formula of the special theory holds for
whatever is built from the rows alone, and the energy $E$ of a row is
$pc$, $p$ its momentum. A
body acts on the block through four readings, each declared in the GameBoard's frame: the drive ($\mathbf p$ into a pace), the turn (the
content into phase steps per self-creation), the push (the first moment
of the arriving rows into a change of $\mathbf p$) and the count (the
rows met, by the crossing rule, into the owed count). A body all of
whose readings are covariant operations on the block's limit inherits
the block's symmetry; a reading declared per interval, per Link or along
the axes breaks it at the order at which the frame enters
\cite{derivations}. This is the theorem (proved there for the linear
block; the rest is a checklist), and it says exactly where Lorentz is
and is not in the law.

\paragraph{Built on Newton.} All four readings give Newton's laws at $v
\ll c$, and each is registered: inertia (G2's thrown stars keep their
momentum, $1.0000$), $F = ma$ ($\mathrm{push}_m = m \times
\mathrm{push}_1$, series C), the third law to the grain, and $GM/r^2$
with one $G$ (series E's shell mean, a reading of probes on the
GameBoard; series D closes no orbit). The law's own correction to Newton is the drive's,
first order in $v/c$ where nature's is second; the covariant drive below
has the right order \cite{derivations}.

\paragraph{Tried on Lorentz.} As declared, three of the four readings
are in the GameBoard's frame, and the consequences are those listed: a
body's own counter counts intervals at the rate $1$ at every speed (the
program's muon world would read its $64$th turn at the interval $64$; a
pin, not run); the dispersion is $v = p/(m + p/c)$, the pace $0.632c$
against $0.864c$ at $p = m$ in the map's units; the push is the retarded
flux along the retarded direction, one factor of $1/\gamma$ short of the
field the magnetic factor multiplies, so a bound orbit thrown at $0.21c$
contracts by $0.87$ to $0.96$ and slows by $1.31$ to $1.43$ where
Lorentz gives $0.977$ and $1.024$; and Einstein's 1905 argument run with
the law's count gives $\Delta m = 0$, no inertia of energy. The
fastest coasting star of the program's Hubble world reads the redshift
$z = 0.2636$ where the classical value is $0.2674$ and $\gamma$'s
$0.315$ (registered, series G2). A bond kept by rows exchanged at $c$
slows by $\gamma^2$ along and $\gamma$ across, the classical ether's
light clock with nothing to contract, so a laboratory moving through the GameBoard would show a two-way anisotropy of order $\beta^2/2$, which
the isotropy measurements refute unless the laboratory is at rest in
the GameBoard's frame (Table~\ref{tab:nature}, row 5b). Against these
stand the measurements: the muon's lifetime at $\gamma = 29.33$
\cite{bailey1977}, time dilation on stored ions to $2 \times
10^{-9}$ \cite{botermann2014}, the isotropy of $c$ to $10^{-18}$
\cite{nagel2015}; the electron's speed against its kinetic energy
\cite{bertozzi1964} the law does not reach, having no energy of motion.

\paragraph{The covariant readings.} For each reading its covariant form
exists within the six operations and without a root, in the form the
physics-rule review left after its nine must-fixes (the derivation's
17.6): the self-creation of a body gated by a proper-time owed count,
an accumulator gaining $\Eint - m$ against the wall $m$ with its
remainder kept ($\Eint$ the body's energy in mass units, an integer,
named below, $m$ its rest value), so that self-creations come one per $\Eint/m =
\gamma$ intervals and everything
counted per self-creation (the age, the turn, the release, the crossing
count) follows proper time at once; the push as the gradient of the age
moment across the six Ports, $-\nabla\mathcal A$ alone; the energy
carried not as an accumulator of the work but as the exact square $E^2/c^4 =
m^2 + 3\,\mathbf p\cdot\mathbf p$ on the record ($c^2$ declared as
the pair $[1, 3]$), with $\Eint$ the largest integer whose square is at most $E^2/c^4$,
kept by comparisons only; and the
pace $pc^2/E$ Links per interval, the covariant $\mathbf v = \mathbf p
c^2/E$ exactly, capped at $1/\sqrt3$ as $p$ grows. Under them $m =
N_l N_w M$, that is $E_0 = mc^2$ with $m$ the mass in label units, is forced by the Newtonian limit; the invariant $E^2/c^4 -
3\,\mathbf p\cdot\mathbf p = m^2$ is exact by construction, $\Eint$ its
integer root to one unit (the open item of the floating-point check
closed); on a change of content the square gains $N_l N_w(2m + N_l N_w\,\mathrm dM)\,\mathrm dM$, so the
inertia of energy and the mass defect are identities of the record; the
identity $3hn = N_l N_w d$ ties the cost $h$ of a phase step, the turn's
pair $n/d$, the width and the grain to the pace and is checked at load.
The second round of the review (buildable with must-fixes, six
sentences, all made) declared the identity's domain and cadence: the
pace $pc^2/E$ holds while the momentum's Manhattan length is at most $N_l N_w M$ ($\gamma$ at most $2$, $\beta$ at most $0.866$, on a heading;
refused at load beyond it);
the push per interval is capped at the grain, so $\Eint$ is kept by at
most three comparisons per interval; the crossing count is summed on
the record between self-creations and charged at the self-creation,
so the count per self-creation is $\gamma$ times the moving reader's
count $1 - \mathbf n\cdot\boldsymbol\beta$ per direction; the release advances per interval of the GameBoard, so a body's field
is its own energy per interval (the probe's pin, $1$ at rest and
$\gamma$ in motion, stands); the load-time identity is a diagnostic,
not a refusal, except where a world declares the exchange's books; and
the sentence that the exchange's books balance is
withdrawn until derived (a row's energy on the heading's $c$ and the
square on the pair $[1, 3]$ leave a gap no rate closes)
\cite{derivations,log}. Figure~\ref{fig:square} shows the identity's
energy form beside Einstein's. What the readings do not give: the
contraction. The gradient of the age
moment alone gives, on a co-moving pair, the rest force along the
motion and $\gamma$ times it across, where Maxwell's are $1 - \beta^2$
and $1/\gamma$; the magnetic part of the push needs the source's
velocity, which no row carries and no reading of a Node or its six
neighbours gives, so Lorentz's 1904 pair is not derived from this
identity, and a row carrying its source's momentum label
(source-velocity-v1) is named for it, not designed
\cite{derivations}. The rules are one hypothesis, covariant-readings-v1,
passing the program's three tests on paper (no family name, one of the
six operations, the record and its six neighbours), reviewed as
admissible with must-fixes and amended accordingly \cite{log}; not
built, not run; every moving body's register moves under them. Open
under them, in one place: the build and the run (form B decided and in
build, not on main); the domain, $\gamma$ at most $2$ on a heading; the
contraction and the exchange's books, withdrawn until derived; and a
moving detector's $c$, which no derivation or run gives one-way or by
round trip (a one-way count $1 \mp \beta$, the two-way count of an
isotropic crowd the rest count exactly, the round-trip time $\gamma^2$
along and $\gamma$ across; Table~\ref{tab:nature}, row 5b).
